TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The United States has operated a military base—now the Pituffik Space Base—in Greenland since the United States and Denmark first signed a defense treaty in 1951. In his Friday announcement, Trump said that “from now on, no U.S. adversary can EVER have a base in Greenland, have a military presence in Greenland, or make sensitive investments in Greenland, without our express written approval.” This could give the United States virtually unilateral control over Greenland’s vast critical minerals and rare-earth suppl

* It’s Time to Update the U.N. Sustainable Development Goals
  Foreign Policy | 21 Sep 19:44 UTC
  Countries committed to end poverty. They never accounted for forever wars.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. World leaders are about to choose a new United Nations secretary-general. Whoever gets the job will inherit an uncomfortable truth: The world body’s promise to end poverty, hunger, and disease is in retreat because today’s leaders are presiding over an era of multiple and growing forever wars. In September 2015, U.N. member states set 17 Sustainable Development Goals, or SDGs, to be achieved by 2030. These sought to end extreme poverty, promote climate action, and set social and economic targets, all while “leaving no one behind.” But the commitment assumed the existence of functioning states, international cooperation, and development systems capable of turning finance into progress. World leaders are about to choose a new United Nations secretary-general. Whoever gets the job will inherit an uncomfortable truth: The world body’s promise to end poverty, hunger, and disease is in retreat because today’s leaders are presiding over an era of multiple and growing forever wars. In September 2015, U.N. member states set 17 Sustainable Development Goals, or SDGs, to be achieved by 2030. These sought to end extreme poverty, promote climate action, and set social and economic targets, all while “leaving no one behind.” But the commitment assumed the existence of functioning states, international cooperation, and development systems capable of turning finance into progress. Instead, the world has entered a new global disorder . States flout laws, and impunity is on the rise, while aid budgets are cut and international cooperation is undermined. The United States, which was once the anchor of the global system, has abandoned its former role. Countries including the United Kingdom have joined in with significant aid cuts. Meanwhile, more wars are raging today than at any point since World War II, and they are lasting longer than ever. More than 70 percent of conflicts that have begun since 2020 have continued for more than a year, up from 44 percent in the 2000s. So-called civil wars, or wars within states, are increasingly internationalized through external sponsorship. According to the Institute for Economics & Peace’s 2025 Global Peace Index , 98 countries were entangled in conflicts outside of their borders in the five years prior to the report, up from 59 in 2008. These internationalized wars are nearly four times less likely to end. The U.S. war of choice in Iran exemplifies the adage that it is easier to start a war t
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/21/united-nations-sustainable-development-goals-sdgs-conflict-aid/

* Nuclear Energy Is the Kremlin’s Latest Leverage Over Washington
  Foreign Policy | 21 Sep 15:22 UTC
  The United States has done little to end its dependence on Russia for enriched uranium.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Between 1995 and 2013, the uranium from around 20,000 Soviet nuclear warheads supplied up to 10 percent of U.S. electricity. After the Cold War arms race, the Megatons to Megawatts program turned 500 metric tons of bomb-grade uranium from decommissioned Soviet-era nuclear weapons into low-enriched uranium (LEU) for U.S. nuclear reactors. Today, Russia remains America’s top supplier of enrichment services (the process that turns natural uranium into nuclear fuel). This is odd: Washington banned imports of Russian LEU in August 2024, a move that prompted Moscow to outlaw such exports to the United States three months later. Both bans appear to be theater, and Moscow’s leverage over Washington on uranium supplies could be more powerful than U.S. officials would like to admit. A brief look at the past helps make sense of U.S. reliance on Russian uranium. During its 18-year time span, the Megatons to Megawatts program ran like clockwork. The conversion and dilution of weapons-grade uranium to reactor fuel took place in four facilities across Russia. Shipments then made their way to Paducah, Kentucky, to complete the process. The $12 billion program was a resounding success, eliminating more than a quarter of the global stock of bomb-grade uranium. Yet this success came at a steep long-term price. Decades of reliance on Russia resulted in chronic underinvestment in U.S. uranium enrichment capacity. Paducah, America’s sole domestically owned enrichment plant, ceased operating when Megatons to Megawatts wound down. The only commercial-scale enrichment facility operating in the United States today belongs to Urenco, a British-Dutch-German firm. Between 1995 and 2013, the uranium from around 20,000 Soviet nuclear warheads supplied up to 10 percent of U.S. electricity. * What Comes After the 1.5 Degree Era
  Foreign Policy | 21 Sep 12:55 UTC
  A single global number asked the world to do everything everywhere all at once—and delivered too little.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The United Nations reported recently that global warming will soon exceed 1.5 degrees C elsius, the goal nations agreed to strive toward in the 2015 Paris Agreement. The best plausible outcome now is a peak near 1.8 degrees C elsius. Most projections are far worse. Scientists have concluded that even 1.5 degrees of sustained warming brings severe consequences (including extreme storms, fires, and heat), and that above it, the risk of unmanageable, irreversible damage rises sharply. Overshooting means much larger investments will be required to prepare for now-inevitable climate disasters. The United Nations reported recently that global warming will soon exceed 1.5 degrees C elsius, the goal nations agreed to strive toward in the 2015 Paris Agreement. The best plausible outcome now is a peak near 1.8 degrees C elsius. Most projections are far worse. Scientists have concluded that even 1.5 degrees of sustained warming brings severe consequences (including extreme storms, fires, and heat), and that above it, the risk of unmanageable, irreversible damage rises sharply. Overshooting means much larger investments will be required to prepare for now-inevitable climate disasters. Yet this failure creates an opening. It clarifies that in the decades to come—decades that matter—not all solutions limit warming equally. Instead of trying to do everything everywhere all at once, nations need to prioritize certain things in certain places right now to limit the damage. To start with, the fastest gains are from cutting super pollutants like methane and ending tropical deforestation. And because some overshoot is now certain, protecting people from what is already coming can no longer be an afterthought. A demonstrator holds a banner showing the 1.5 degree Celsius mark outside the Reichstag building in Berlin, Germany, on April 18. Omer Messinger/Getty Images The global climate goal should not change. Sustained warming above 1.5 degrees Celsius could prove catastrophic . The overshoot must be temporary , with nations bringing temperatures back below 1.5 degrees within a century. Lowering temperatures will require removing carbon dioxide from the air on a vast scale through reforestation and new technologies that are only just emerging. But between now and midcentury, the priority should be different: limiting how far above 1.5 degrees temperatures go, and how long they stay there. The height of the peak matters because parts of 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/21/climate-week-change-after-paris/

* Taiwan’s Status Is Ambiguous. U.S. Policy Shouldn’t Be.
  Foreign Policy | 21 Sep 11:51 UTC
  It’s time for the U.S. to embrace strategic clarity.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For generations, significant elements of international policy have turned on a concerted global effort to ignore an obvious truth: that Taiwan’s legal status is ambiguous. Beijing is adamant that it is sovereign over the island. Taipei is adamant that it is not. Domestic political pressures prevent either from allowing any appearance of giving any ground. Neither is able to unilaterally impose its view on the other; even a “successful” invasion of Taiwan would devastate China’s economy and quite possibly break its military, while a Taiwanese declaration of independence would almost certainly prompt Beijing to demolish the entire island despite the costs and risks. Most of the rest of the world has sought to accommodate both maximalist positions at the same time, largely by formally accepting Beijing’s view while de facto adopting Taipei’s. By focusing on public rhetoric rather than underlying interests and actions, unfortunately, these efforts ultimately produced decades of miscommunication, uncertainty, and dangerous crises. Vitalii Kim, Ukraine’s newly appointed minister for veterans’ affairs, has a job few would envy: helping Ukraine’s over 1 million veterans while operating on a shoestring budget, in a war where the shooting is far from over. Ukraine has to “build the engine while it’s driving the car,” he told Foreign Policy last week while visiting the United States to speak with U.S. officials and nongovernmental organizations. After over four years of war, Ukraine has over 1 million veterans, many of whom suffer from psychological trauma as well as physical injuries, including tens of thousands with missing limbs. The number of veterans following the war’s end could hit 3 million , out of a prewar population of 44 million that has shrunk to under 36 million over the course of the war. But unlike in the United States, where veterans can return home to a society largely unaffected by war, Ukrainian veterans are living in a war-torn country. Continued Russian attacks are slowing the country’s economic growth while posing a threat to the lives of soldiers who once served on the front line. The stakes are existential for Ukraine’s economy. “With their families, [veterans] account for 30 percent of the labor force,” Kim said. “We cannot lose them.” But that challenge could also help solve some of the country’s economic problems, he said, with many of its service members now proven leaders. “We have people with unique skills—we will use these skills in our economy,” Kim said. Besides traditional problems, such as medical care, there are unique challenges borne from the fact that many soldiers were civilians up until 2022. In some cases, this may help Ukraine—many soldiers had a civilian career they could possibly return to. However, soldiers in combat roles can also earn thousands of dollars in pay per month, versus an average salary of less than $1,000 per month in Ukraine. “This is a problem; we need to adjust the situation,” Kim said. “We are preparing programs that will stimulate business to pay [veterans] more.” Kim’s min

  FULL TEXT: This year’s so-called “super El Niño” is entering into record-breaking territory. Sea surface temperatures in the tropical Pacific now equal the previous daily record set in 2015 and will likely keep rising in the days ahead. El Niño is a naturally occurring climate phenomenon in the Pacific that reshapes weather patterns around the world and temporarily boosts global temperatures. The current El Niño event – which has been underway since June and is expected to last until next year – has been developing faster than any previous event on record. The strength of an event is tracked using the “ Niño 3.4 anomaly ”, which measures how much warmer sea surface temperatures in a section of the central Pacific are than average. As of 19 September, the daily anomaly in the Niño 3.4 region stands at 3.07C, putting it in a statistical tie with the previous record of 3.08C. Some scientists, using a different baseline for calculating the anomaly, have already called the new record. Either way, this is remarkable, in part because of how early in the calendar year it is occurring. El Niño typically peaks in the winter months, most commonly in November or December. Every strong El Niño on record has continued to strengthen after mid-September – and there is every reason to think that this one will as well. (For more on El Niño, see Carbon Brief’s recent interactive explainer .) El Niño events are typically classed as “weak” when the Niño 3.4 anomaly reaches 0.5C, “moderate” above 1C, “strong” above 1.5C and “very strong” above 2.0C. For this year’s event, the Niño 3.4 anomaly has now reached 3.07C, which puts it in a statistical tie with the record set on 18 November 2015, set during a “very strong” El Nino event . The chart below shows how the strength of the current El Niño (red line) is dramatically outpacing both 2015-16 (blue) and another “very strong” event in 1997-98 (light blue). To analyse the developing El Niño, Carbon Brief followed the convention of the US National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration’s (NOAA) Oceanic Niño index (ONI). ONI is calculated by subtracting the latest 30-year average temperature in the Nino 3.4 region from daily sea surface temperatures. This approach allows for the most recent years to be compared against the most recent 30-year period. It removes much of the influence of longer-term, human-driven warming from the index. (While meteorological organisations typically track changes to ONI on a three-month rolling average basis, Car
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-super-el-nino-reaches-remarkable-all-time-record

* Europe’s Central Bank Prepares to Invest Own Funds in Tokenized Securities
  Decrypt | 21 Sep 19:00 UTC
  The central bank plans to buy euro-denominated public-sector debt and settle the transactions through its new Pontes service.
  FULL TEXT: The European Central Bank has begun preparing to invest a small portion of its own funds in tokenized securities, with the transactions settling through its newly launched Pontes service. In an announcement on Monday, the ECB said the purchases would help it “gain practical experience as an investor and build institutional expertise in the use of distributed ledger technology (DLT) in financial markets.” “Tokenised financial markets continue to evolve, alongside the development of new DLT-based market infrastructures,” the European Central Bank said in a statement. “By investing directly, the ECB will gain first-hand experience across the full investment lifecycle, including trade execution, settlement, systems and portfolio management activities.” Initial purchases will focus on tokenized, euro-denominated securities issued by euro-area governments, public agencies, and European supranational institutions. The assets—conventional securities such as bonds issued or recorded on a distributed ledger—will be settled in central bank money through the Eurosystem’s Pontes service. The investments will come from the ECB’s own-funds portfolio, a non-monetary-policy portfolio that generates income to help cover operating expenses unrelated to its supervisory duties. The ECB has not disclosed the exact amount it plans to invest or when purchases will begin. Its Executive Board will determine the operational details and timing after the preparatory work is complete, taking into account developments in tokenized issuances and Europe’s broader tokenized financial ecosystem. Pontes is part of a Eurosystem strategy to “make central bank money fit for the digital age.” It will operate alongside Appia, an initiative developing a blueprint for a tokenized financial ecosystem in Europe. “Pontes will allow market participants, including those who took part in the exploratory work, to further enhance their services, thereby supporting market developments while preserving the anchoring role of central bank money,” the bank wrote . The ECB said Pontes will combine three tested systems into one service, allowing transactions to settle with cash tokens or through the Eurosystem’s T2 payment system. Its Hash-Link protocol will synchronize asset transfers and payments, while automated processing will reduce manual work. Public and private institutions are pursuing similar projects. In May, JPMorgan filed for a tokenized Ethereum money-market fund . In June, New York Life Investment 
  https://decrypt.co/378872/europe-central-bank-invest-funds-tokenized-securities

## EDITORIAL

--- Editorial (37) ---

* On Not Being a Homeowner
  Longreads | 21 Sep 17:34 UTC
  "Generational wealth shapes our futures and friendships, whether we talk about it or not."
  FULL TEXT: In this personal essay at The New Yorker , Anna Holmes writes about growing up in Davis, California, a college town near Sacramento; the financial challenges her mixed-race family faced; and what it means to come from a family without the generational wealth that can make homeownership, and the financial security that comes with it, possible. It’s hard to hear people who own property grumble about how little money they have. One friend who regularly moans about money has a four-bedroom home on the West Coast and a vacation house in New England. I’m unmoved by the financial lamentations of another friend, who owns a town house in New York with an apartment for a nanny or an in-law. Stuff like this makes me want to scream. Demonstrate some modicum of self-awareness! Read the fucking room! When a friend told me that her parents were going to help her buy a second home, I made a pointed remark that it must be nice to have access to that sort of capital. The deportation and resulting trauma were the result of an Asylum Cooperative Agreement that the Ugandan government signed with the United States in July 2025. The tomatoes do not have opinions, or so I tell myself, though it is mostly a lie. For they do lean toward the sun and then, perversely, away from it. They sulk when I overwater and they sulk when I forget, and they have firm convictions about how much attention is too much. But whatever they want, they show me plainly. A leaning plant wants turning. A pale, unhappy one wants trimming. A bushy one wants care. There is no tone in it—no door slamming or softly clicking shut upstairs, no message that says fine in a way that is clearly not fine. When a tomato is unhappy with me, it tells me in a language I can actually answer. And it does tell me. Skip a few days—a work trip, a long weekend, some obligation that drags me out of the side yard—and they turn unruly, almost insolent. “The bed of Lake Superior is part museum, part graveyard.” “Nature’s networks, fluidity, and diversity are the keys to our future.”

* He was probably right
  Aeon | 21 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Cicero’s life and thought are a testament to the idea of probabilia: we should be confident in our beliefs, but never certain - by Massimo Pigliucci Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A statue of Cicero at the Capitoline Museums, Rome, Italy. Photo by Alessandra Benedetti/Corbis via Getty Images How tightly should you hold your beliefs? You face the question several times a week without noticing. Hold them too loosely and you don’t stand for anything, hold them too tightly and you become the sort of person no argument can reach. Most of us swing between the two and have no vocabulary for the ground in between. A Roman lawyer worked one out, under pressure far worse than anything we are likely to face, and it is still the best answer available. The time is early 45 BCE, the place a villa near Lucrine Lake, northwest of Naples, in southern Italy. One of the most fascinating and controversial figures in the entirety of Western history, Marcus Tullius Cicero, is finishing writing a book that will be known as the Academica , where he explains the nature of sceptical philosophy and why it is so crucial not just to the advancement of human knowledge, but to living a good life. It is a strange moment to retire into one’s villa to write. The Roman Republic, so beloved by Cicero, is about to be destroyed by aspiring autocrats: Julius Caesar, a longtime friend and now a political opponent, has declared himself dictator for life, a move that will soon lead to his assassination on the Ides of March 44 BCE. On the personal front, Cicero has recently divorced Terentia, his wife of 30 years, and a few months ago his beloved daughter Tullia died from complications following childbirth. You would think a man whose private and public worlds are collapsing so dramatically would hardly have the focus to write philosophy. Yet for Cicero such writing is a form of therapy, and the treatises he produces will influence medieval Christians, Renaissance authors, and the Founders of the American Republic. C icero managed all this because of his adopted philosophy, ‘Academic Skepticism’, so-called because it characterised, for the period from 266 to 90 BCE, the Academy originally founded by Plato. He used to be treated as a mere populariser, a gifted Roman relaying Greek ideas he did not really own, but scholars abandoned that uncharitable verdict decades ago as it became clear that Cicero argued and decided among his sources rather than simply transcribing them. He also built a Latin philosophical vocabulary from scratch, and much of the abstract terminology of European thought descends from his coinages. On the question that mattered most to his own school – wheth
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-good-sceptic-has-more-than-doubt-and-less-than-certainty

* The case for a robot tax to redistribute wealth
  Rest of World | 18 Sep 10:00 UTC
  In an excerpt from his book, “Innovate for Impact: A Roadmap to Sustainable Technology Beyond AI,” Alessandro Crimi explains that a tax on automation is more effective than retraining labor.
  FULL TEXT: AI has the potential to revolutionize economies by opening up new avenues for innovation, production, education, and problem solving. It also has the potential to increase current disparities and inequalities , and exacerbate environmental issues if it is not addressed critically. Integrating AI-driven innovation with social ideals that place a high priority on shared wealth, a just society, and ecological balance is a very difficult task. Retraining programs are a necessary but insufficient response to systemic displacement. A critical warning is being sounded that automation risks displacing labor and depressing wages unless addressed with safety nets and reallocation programs. Even in medieval times, which are rarely considered times of innovation, we had some sort of policies to reallocate people. The guild system, churches, and monasteries were training centers. Innovations like the windmill, or the watermill, created a demand for millwrights, who trained assistants informally. However, there is a reason why we remember medieval times as bleak; even with the retraining of people, innovations did not translate into improved living standards for the majority. Therefore, it is worth focusing on retraining policies, as well as profit sharing , taxation, and social safety nets as potential solutions for AI’s disruptions. With AI, although potential compensatory mechanisms such as new tasks and productivity gains are promised, history cautions us that such benefits are neither automatic nor equitably shared. The conversation around AI and labor, therefore, must broaden from a singular focus on retraining displaced workers to encompassing strategies for redistributing the wealth that automation creates, and interrogating the physical substrate upon which this new economy is built. Retraining programs are a necessary but insufficient response to systemic displacement. They place the full burden of adaptation on the individual and often fail to match the velocity of change. Structural policies that transform economic relationships are required to complement them, such as safety nets, reduction of working hours or universal basic income policies. The four-day workweek policy reduces the standard working week (usually to 32 hours) without cutting pay, aiming to improve the balance between work and home, productivity and mental health. UBI is a policy in which every adult receives a regular unconditional cash payment from the state to guarantee basic economic sec
  https://restofworld.org/2026/robot-tax-ai-wealth-redistribution/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* Cosmic amnesia
  Aeon | 18 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A black hole rings like a struck bell, then settles into silence, forgetting almost everything about its history. Why? - by Richard Dyer Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Artist’s impression of GW250114, the celestial collision of two black holes, observed in gravitational waves by the US National Science Foundation LIGO. Courtesy Aurore Simonnet (SSU/EdEon)/LVK/URI/LIGO/CALTECH It is 9 am on a warm July Sunday in Cambridge, as I head towards a café in the city centre. The bells of Great St Mary’s church play the familiar melody of the Cambridge Quarters . The sound, now often associated with the chimes of Big Ben in London, was first composed here in 1793. The quarters, named for the melody at each quarter-hour, are an immediately recognisable reminder of the passage of time. Each bell produces a note: a wave with a characteristic frequency that travels through the air and reaches our ears. A hammer strikes the bell, and it oscillates, pushing air molecules back and forth. Those jostling molecules reach our ears and we hear a musical note. Then the bell settles, gives up its energy, and returns to silence. My research here at the University of Cambridge often brings me back to the ringing of a bell. I don’t study the physics of sound, but I do focus on a remarkably similar process, taking place throughout the Universe. I am interested in another object that also rings: a black hole. As I sit down with a coffee, I open my laptop to a wavy signal that gradually fades to a flat line. Two black holes have finished merging – a frenzied process of coalescence – leaving an extremely brief wiggle originating from the remnant black hole. Much like the struck bell, this leftover black hole is ringing in a process called ringdown , and it’s consistent across every merger I investigate. Ringdown allows physicists to test their theories of gravity: did Albert Einstein really have the last word? Just as an off-sounding note from a bell might indicate a design flaw, a discrepancy in the frequencies of a ringing black hole could be the first crack in our understanding of gravity. That’s the scientific rationale for studying ringdown, but what fascinates me equally is what happens next. After the black hole rings, it settles into silence. In that moment, lasting only a few milliseconds, the power and intensity of the merger are lost. From the exterior, almost all of that history suddenly becomes inaccessible, and what is left is featureless. The black hole settles into a simple, peaceful object of pure spacetime, with no trace of the violence that just happened. In some respects, the black holes forget . It seems counterintuitive that some
  https://aeon.co/essays/black-holes-ring-like-bells-what-happens-next-is-stranger

More than 1,400 civilians were killed at the time, according to Syria’s National Commission of Inquiry, which was tasked with establishing the facts surrounding the coastal violence. Some of the killings were summary executions, several of which were filmed and circulated on social media. It was the first major episode of violence to erupt since the fall of Assad, in a predominantly Alawite coastal region and the former regime’s stronghold. Eight defendants were accused of taking part in the rebellion, one of whom never showed up to court. Eight others, including one civilian and seven members of government forces, were accused of violence against civilians. The National Commission of Inquiry identified 298 potential perpetrators of violations against Alawite civilians, as well as 265 people potentially involved in attacks against government forces. Eighteen months after the violence, after more than a dozen hearings and 10 months of proceedings, the court was now delivering its first verdicts. At the center of the courtroom, Civil Judge Zakaria Bakkar, assisted by two military advisers, began to read in the monotonous, neutral voice that has characterized the hearings. Shafik was found guilty of participating in an armed gang formed with the purpose of committing killings, looting and theft. He was sentenced to life imprisonment. During previous hearings, Shafik had admitted to stealing military vehicles as events unfolded, and handing them over to people who allegedly later sold them in Idlib, Aleppo and Hama.   FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Gallo Images/Orbital Horizon/Copernicus Sentinel Data 2026) More than 11 years after Saudi Arabia launched its military intervention in Yemen, the Houthi front has once again confronted Riyadh with the same strategic dilemma, only under more dangerous regional conditions. Saudi Arabia has recently built new security arrangements, including the Mecca Joint Defence Agreement with Turkey and Pakistan and a multinational maritime initiative focused on the Red Sea and Bab al-Mandab (Pakistan said on Thursday the pact ought to be activated following the Houthi attacks). Yet neither has so far produced a visible response to the present crisis. It may still be too early to judge these arrangements, but the current escalation is already becoming their first serious test. The deeper question for Riyadh is how to redefine security in a way that protects not only its borders, but also its energy infrastructure, trade routes and wider regional interests. The escalation cannot be separated from what has happened to Iran elsewhere in the region. Tehran’s influence has contracted in Syria and Lebanon, while its networks in Iraq have come under greater pressure amid a broader confrontation with the United States and Israel. In that context, Yemen has become more valuable. The Houthis are not simply an Iran-aligned force on Saudi Arabia’s border; they are a military actor capable of pressuring the kingdom in the Red Sea while Iran uses Hormuz as a bargaining chip. As helicopters pass overhead and a suspected kill pen mysteriously catches fire, suspicion mounts among the troops, and interpretations diverge. A genuinely surprising portrait of a loose, wayward movement, delivered with peak dramatic tension. This was the Ostrich Army way: every event called for interpretation, an attempt to see through to hidden endgames. “One thing with black magic,” one camper would later say to me, “is they tell you what they’re going to do before they do it.” And so, at first light, when firefighters had won the day and the smoke cleared to reveal the ostrich flock still standing, the sight didn’t lead to any reconsideration of the night’s theories of clandestine slaughter. The Army simply moved on, hoping to make better sense of the next move, or the one after that. The birds really could die this day. “Millions of songbirds in Southeast Asia are trapped and smuggled each year for keeping as pets or entering in competitions. * South Africa joins the global resistance against American data centers
  Rest of World | 17 Sep 10:00 UTC
  “A massive data center is trying to jump the queue and take our land, water, and energy.”
  FULL TEXT: Civil rights groups in South Africa are protesting the expansion of data centers by American companies, marking one of the first coordinated attempts on the continent to slow AI infrastructure development. “What we are saying is there should be a moratorium on data centers. … It must be across South Africa,” Kashiefa Achmat, chairperson of Housing Assembly, a Cape Town-based advocacy group, told Rest of World . South Africa has the most data centers on the continent, owned predominantly by American hyperscalers like Microsoft, Amazon, and Google, and data center operators such as Equinix and Digital Realty. They are racing to fill the continent’s rising demand for compute, which could hit 2.2 gigawatts by 2030, about five times the current levels, according to a McKinsey report. Microsoft has committed 5.4 billion rand ($300 million) by 2027 to expand its digital and AI infrastructure in South Africa. Amazon Web Services will invest $1.5 billion until 2029 to expand AI capabilities across Africa. In South Africa, Equinix plans to invest $438 million. “This is a strategic moment for African countries precisely because companies want access to new markets and locations,” Rachel Adams, founder of the Global Center on AI Governance, an African think tank on AI, told Rest of World . The expansion comes as data center projects globally face scrutiny over their environmental impact. In the U.S., residents have rallied against these facilities in Virginia, California, and Philadelphia. In June, New York imposed a statewide moratorium for up to a year on hyperscale data centers. Protests have also been seen elsewhere in the world, including by farmers in India and Indigenous groups in Brazil . Housing Assembly and U.K.-based nonprofit Foxglove filed a legal challenge in August against two data centers proposed by Equinix in Cape Town. Along with other rights groups, they called on the South African Human Rights Commission to investigate the projects’ impacts and impose a pause on hyperscale data center expansion. They have also called for new regulations requiring operators to disclose their water and electricity usage, and provide benefits to local communities. At the center of the protest is California-based Equinix, one of the world’s largest data center operators, which provides facilities to hyperscalers and other clients. It has benefited from the AI boom, with its market capitalization rising over 30% since last December to $99.36 billion as of September 16.

In this presentation of events—a “scum of the earth” client, in his words, and a terrific guy who snapped, as anyone would have—Barisone has enjoyed near-universal support from the dressage community, and beyond. In much of the online discussion about the case, including in the forums of The Chronicle of the Horse , a long-established magazine, commenters have identified two victims, not one, and have almost always found Barisone’s victimhood the more compelling. A woman was shot, but a man was driven to shoot her, because she was annoying, and at times belligerent, in person and on social media. Kanarek has been widely scorned as a dressage interloper and as an embodiment of contemporary ills: a bullshitter, an entitled troll. “A polo legend and a businessman joined forces to copy the player’s greatest horse. His country’s flag does not fly from the masts on the plaza outside the U.N. headquarters in Manhattan, nor does his delegation receive diplomatic security. Yet Somaliland, the country he leads, is very real. This statelet of some 6 million people has sought recognition from the outside world since 1991, when it broke away from Somalia, so far to no avail. That changed last December, when Israel became the first country to offer recognition. Israel’s alleged motive for the recognition was to gain a military foothold on the Gulf of Aden, just across from the Houthis; but this did not prevent Somalilanders from seeing the moment as a turning point in their history. At last, an influential state was recognizing Somaliland’s decades-long efforts to build a stable state and a functioning democracy, as well as the strategic importance of its location overlooking the Bab al-Mandab Strait, one of the world’s major trade routes. Now, Abdullahi, known colloquially as “Irro,” is coming for the ultimate prize: U.S. recognition. That outcome is far from certain. The Somaliland delegation knows this, so it’s taking plenty of time to make its case. Abdullahi arrived in the U.S. on Sept. 5 and will spend the entire month in New York and Washington. “Our goal is to meet as many lawmakers and top officials as possible. The stakes for us are very high,” said Mohammed Abdirahman, director general of Somaliland’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs and International Cooperation. Abdullahi’s team is being deliberately vague about the “top officials”: They are hoping to secure a meeting with Secretary of State Marco Rubio or President Donald Trump but are still not sure they will succeed. The long-shot meeting has been in the works for months. * In Dubai, Trade Continues on the Front Lines of the Iran War
  New Lines | 15 Sep 10:44 UTC
  After more than six months of war with Iran, the UAE has vowed to stop all trade with its neighbor. Yet fruit and vegetables from Iran still reach the Gulf states daily, showing how tightly the region’s economies are bound together. The post In Dubai, Trade Continues on the Front Lines of the Iran War appeared first on New Lines Magazine ...
  FULL TEXT: Rashed Aljabri swings a watermelon from the truck to his colleague standing on the floor of Al Aweer, Dubai’s largest fruit and vegetable market. After building up a pyramid-like stack, he slices one in half with a knife and cuts out a scarlet segment from the center. “Iran, Iran, Iran,” Aljabri chants. It is 6 a.m., and pallet trucks loaded with cauliflowers, boxes of tomatoes, bulbous pumpkins, eggplants and broccoli in ice-filled foam boxes whiz around the wholesale market, narrowly avoiding beeping tuk-tuks in near-orchestrated harmony. “Ninety percent of the produce in this market is from Iran,” Aljabri estimates. Containers marked with Tehran addresses reverse into the docking bays, traders unloading trolleys of salad and celery. More than six months into the war with Iran, with no end in sight, the United Arab Emirates is once again ramping up pressure on its neighbor across the Gulf, announcing last month that it would halt all trade ties. The move, announced in response to Iranian strikes on Emirati ships, is seen as part of the Trump administration’s strategy of economically strangling Iran. But Iranian watermelons, and much more, are still flowing into Dubai’s markets, feeding its restaurants, supermarkets and hotels, and providing one small example of how difficult it would be to fully separate the two countries’ historically linked economies. Although the Strait of Hormuz has today become a geopolitical choke point, blocking the flow of oil, fertilizer and global trade, the area has long been a local hub of economic and cultural exchange. For centuries, merchants from Iran and the Arabian Peninsula sailed across the Gulf on wooden dhows, ferrying saffron, pearls, spices and pottery. Over the years, communities in the ports of Dubai, Bandar Abbas and Bandar Lengeh became a melting pot of Persian and Arabic culture, speaking both languages. Despite the ongoing conflict, this ancient trade is still visible in Dubai. In the old town, impressively large turquoise and white dhows parade the port at Marfa Wharf, cranes loading their cargo, soon ready to sail. In 1979, the Iranian Revolution sparked a huge influx of wealthy Iranian business owners and educated professionals fleeing the new Islamic Republic. Today the UAE is home to some 560,000 Iranians, the vast majority in Dubai. They own businesses, buy property and employ workers, representing a cornerstone of the economy. * Where in the world is the iPhone cheapest?

  Apple’s high-end models cost more in markets like India and Turkey owing to high taxes and import duties.

The two models will cost the highest in Turkey, nearly double the U.S. price, due to local taxes, Counterpoint says. In India — iPhone’s fourth-largest market and the second-largest manufacturing hub for Apple — prices will be 30% to 40% higher than in the U.S. for similar reasons. The steep price tags persist despite Apple’s continued efforts to diversify manufacturing operations to reduce dependence on China. India now accounts for about 25% of Apple’s total global iPhone production, but the new foldable model Duo is not being produced in the country. “India pricing is steep, and the difference is primarily due to the fact that initial Duo sales will not be ‘Made in India’ for some time,” Tarun Pathak, director of devices and ecosystems at Counterpoint Research, told Rest of World . “Consequently, imported units attract full import duty — making them significantly more expensive — along with GST [goods and services tax] and the impact of a weaker rupee.” The U.S., as always, continues to have the most affordable prices in the world for the latest launches. The second-cheapest market, the UAE, has a relatively smaller price tag not just for the iPhone but also for other brands and models, due to a low value-added tax at 5% and no other taxes. 25% of Apple’s global iPhone production is now from India. In China and Korea, prices skew lower not only due to different tax rates but also because of robust local market competition. “China is highly competitive with Huawei, Oppo, Vivo, while Samsung is the heavyweight in Korea,” Harshit Rastogi, senior analyst at Counterpoint Research, told Rest of World . Even the made-in-India iPhones are still largely only assembled in the country. Only 18% to 20% of electronic components originate domestically, while the rest are imported and taxed heavily. “The Pro models have even higher import dependence than the base model,” Rastogi said. “As a greater share of components becomes locally sourced in the coming years, price increases are likely to become more gradual.” In June, Apple’s former CEO Tim Cook warned that the price of existing models would go up due to a shortage of chips. * Chasing la dolce vita
  Aeon | 15 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Homecomings, fantasies, food and ghosts – how the myth of eternal return keeps the Italian diaspora in a state of longing - by Ben Faccini Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: All original watercolours by Emily Faccini When he was a child growing up in Emilia-Romagna, my great-grandfather Marco once found an eagle fledgling that had fallen from its nest. He kept it warm in the folds of his shirt and ran home, only to discover as he undressed that the bird had eaten strips of his flesh, its beak tinged red with blood. The rescued eagle thrived in my great-grandfather’s care, its wingspan soon a remarkable length. And all the while, Marco himself grew immensely tall. Then, one day, without warning, the eagle flew away. The moment my father told me this story when I was a boy, it lodged itself deep in my imagination. I pictured young Marco as a giant striding through the forests of north-central Italy, calling to the bird of prey, waiting in vain for it to land on his outstretched hand. Marco merged with the heroes of my illustrated classical civilisation books, earning his place between the busts of Roman emperors and the chapters on Greek mythology. What I never realised was that this family legend held clues to who I am and to my relationship to Italy. L ina Stores deli in Soho, London, has been a pillar of Italian life in England for decades. It’s where we’d sometimes meet our paternal grandparents when visiting London from our home in France. My brothers and I would line up in front of the refrigerated counter and ask for our favourite ravioli, doing our utmost to avoid any slip-ups in Italian that would make the staff wince: ‘ ricotta e spinaci ’, ‘ zucca ’, ‘ funghi porcini ’. Back at our grandparents’ flat, Nonna served the ravioli on an elaborately laid table, and my grandfather appeared with freshly grated Parmigiano. He’d sprinkle the cheese evenly over our pasta; never too much, nor too little. How he did this, and with such speed, we didn’t know. If we had spaghetti, Nonna would wrap her right hand around ours and twirl the fork with us so we held just the right amount, the bespattered tablecloth damning proof of our slapdash habits at home. My grandparents trusted that these rituals of the table (they saw them as basic codes of Italianness, connecting displaced Italians everywhere) would allow us to paper over the differences in our ethnically blended and peripatetic family. In this way, we’d be able to claim Italian identity for ourselves, despite its competition in our lives with Englishness and Frenchness.
